# Kanea: Product Requirements Document

| | |
|---|---|
| **Status** | Adopted v1.89 |
| **Author** | Michael K. Essandoh (<michael@essandoh.dev>) |
| **Last updated** | 2026-08-20 (v1.89) |
| **Document type** | Product Requirements Document (PRD) |

> **v1.1 amendments**: incorporates the engineering review (performance/reliability/security): edge proxy split into `kanea-edge` (§5.2.6), Store-level CDC replication + master-key escrow (§15.3), upgrade & migration framework (§15.4), workload hardening defaults + CSRF/CSWSH/OIDC hardening (§14), ACME wildcard-default policy (§7.3), metrics pipeline redesign (§9.1), storm controls (§4.3, §11), realistic RTO targets (§15.3, §21), total-platform footprint budget (§21).

> **v1.2 amendments**: adds the **MCP server** (first-class AI-agent interface: §5.2.1, §13.3, §16.3, M9→M10 renumbering) and **edge middleware** on the `expose` block; IP restriction, rate limiting, header manipulation (§5.2.6, §6.1, §7.2, M3).

> **v1.3 amendments**: **image-only deployment** is explicit as the minimal, first-class path (G14, §6.2 R8, CLI quick-run) and adds **service references & dependencies**: `${service.<name>.host}` / `${service.<name>.port.*}` interpolation, `depends_on`, topological health-gated starts, cycle rejection (§6.2 R9-R10, §7.1.1, §4.3).

> **v1.89 amendments**: **the pids cap gets a knob, because 256 is a number not a workload** (§6.2 R11, §5.2.11). Every alloc has carried `pids.max = 256` since M1 as fork-bomb containment, with no spec field to change it, and the first thread-heavy workload met the wall exactly where such walls are met: Typesense aborts during RocksDB open, `pthread_create` answering `EAGAIN` once the RocksDB reopen pool, the brpc/bthread workers and the raft stack have consumed 256 pids between them, and every restart walks recovery into the same wall. The cap's *presence* is not negotiable — a fork bomb is not a workload to accommodate (R12) — but its *value* is a workload property, and a platform that cannot run a stock search engine has the number wrong rather than the workload. `resources { pids = 2048 }` declares a service's own cap. The shape follows the R11 family exactly: a declared value is enforced exactly, a declared zero means what omission means, a negative is refused at plan, and the omitted field keeps the 256 default — the record carries the same `PidsLimit` it has always carried, so **nothing rolls at upgrade**, while declaring a value *is* a spec-hash change and rolls once, because the cap is written into the cgroup at task creation and a deploy is how an alloc acquires a new one. An `init` block takes no `pids` (a step's cap is the alloc's, and has been since R32; the conversion now leaves the step's cap for the reconciler to inherit rather than copying the default in, so a service with init containers rolls once on its next apply — the v1.58 precedent — with the effective cap unchanged) and a `function` block takes none either (R25's sandbox caps are fixed promises: a wasm module cannot fork, and the shim's own thread budget is a runtime property rather than the workload's). R12's accounting in §17 is untouched: admission control sums `cpu`/`memory`, and pids was never an admission unit.

> **v1.88 amendments**: **the CLI ships as a container image, and the image carries the release rather than a rebuild of it** (§5.2.12, §14 A06/A08, §16.2). A pipeline that deploys to a Kanea node needs the CLI on the runner, and the platform had nothing to hand it: the documented CI recipe (§16.2's remote endpoint, `KANEA_URL`/`KANEA_TOKEN`/`KANEA_CA_CERT`) has always been written against `any image with the kanea binary`, which is a way of saying every user builds this themselves, downloads an archive and re-implements the verification `install.sh` already performs. Releases now publish **`ghcr.io/m18h/kanea`**, tagged `vX.Y.Z` and `latest`, `linux/amd64` and `linux/arm64` in one manifest list. **It is a CLI channel, never a node channel**, v1.60's line for Homebrew drawn a second time and for the same reason: `agent`, `edge`, `init`, `install`, `doctor` and `upgrade` act on a host, its systemd, its cgroups and its own binary, and a container is not that host; what the image is for is the `endpointFlags` surface (`plan`, `run`, `deploy`, `ps`, `logs`, `secret`, …) plus `plan`'s parse-and-validate half, which needs no daemon at all. The node story is unchanged: `install.sh` + `kanea init`, root-owned at `/usr/local/bin`, and `kanea upgrade` still refuses a remote endpoint because it swaps the file it is running from. **The load-bearing decision is that the image job runs after the signing job and downloads what that job published**: it fetches the `linux_{amd64,arm64}` archives, verifies `checksums.txt` under the keyless cosign signature and then each archive against it (the two checks `install.sh` performs, in that order), and copies the binary out of the tarball. A second `go build` in a Containerfile would have been simpler to write and would have meant the published `checksums.txt` attested to bytes nobody shipped; as built, the manifest of hashes covers the image's contents with nothing to reconcile, which is v1.30's bundle argument applied to a registry. The **image digest is cosign-signed keyless** with the same GitHub OIDC identity, because a digest is not a file hash and does not belong in `checksums.txt`; adding an asset to carry it would put a fourth name through `install.sh`'s `grep " <archive>$"` selector for no gain. An **SPDX attestation** rides beside the signature (§14 A06): unlike the offline bundles, which are deliberately not catalogued because syft cannot descend into the archives they carry, an image is exactly what syft reads well, so the argument for omitting one does not transfer. **The release workflow still writes only to this repository** (v1.67, reaffirmed rather than weakened): GHCR under `m18h/kanea` is this repository's own package namespace, reached with its own `GITHUB_TOKEN` and `packages: write`, so no cross-repo credential comes into existence; the registry push nonetheless lives in its own job rather than beside `cosign sign-blob`, which is K-42's split, kept for the same reason it was made. The base is **pinned by digest with its tag in a comment** and Dependabot bumps it, because a `FROM` by tag is precisely the mutable-pointer-to-a-root-filesystem §5.2.12 already refuses for the BuildKit component, and a digest nothing ever bumps is a frozen CVE surface rather than a pin. Two details are stated because each reads as arbitrary and is not: the image installs `ca-certificates` (the static binary carries no CA bundle, `--ca-cert` *replaces* the system pool rather than adding to it, so a node behind a public CA needs the bundle even though a self-signed one does not), and it creates a real `/etc/passwd` entry for its non-root user rather than a bare numeric `USER`, since with CGO off `os/user` resolves by parsing that file. Finally, the recipe is **built on every pull request and pushed on none**: a Containerfile first exercised by a tag is a release that fails in the middle, which is the genre §21 keeps recording.

> **v1.87 amendments**: **the service detail page's actions are the ones it has** (§12.2). The row has promised *restart, scale, redeploy, rollback-to-digest* since v1.1 and shipped only restart, which is the v1.71 genre again: a page described by its intentions rather than by its buttons, where the fix is usually the document. Two of the four are now true. **Scale** is a dialog rather than an inline control, because a replica count is a decision and one taken by holding a button down is a decision nobody made deliberately; it is bounded by the service's `scaling` policy where one is declared, mirroring §16.1's refusal exactly, since that route answers an out-of-range count with a 409 rather than clamping it and a control offering the click would hand the operator a guaranteed failure. Scaling to zero stays **Stop's**, which confirms first: two paths to one state is the thing this platform refuses everywhere else. **Open** is new and was not promised: it follows the service's public address, offering a menu when `expose` yields more than one, which happens both when a block lists several domains and when the block itself repeats (v1.50). A route with **no** domains is omitted rather than guessed, because the edge serves it at a name generated under `--base-domain` and reading that needs `GET /v1/settings`, which is admin-only; showing some roles a wrong address is worse than showing nobody a button. The scheme is https unless the route declares plaintext, since R20 resolves TLS node-side and the record cannot say. **Redeploy and rollback-to-digest are recorded as unbuilt** rather than deleted: both want a route that does not exist, since `kanea deploy` round-trips the whole record client-side (v1.82) and no server verb sets an image. Tables page at **20/50/100** rather than 10/20/50/100, and the smallest offered size is also the default, which the pager depends on: it hides itself for a list that fits at the default, so a smaller size would be unreachable on exactly the lists it was meant for. The page's title is now `project/service`, the form every other surface uses, since a service name is unique only inside its project; and the content column is fluid rather than boxed at 1100px, because tables, allocation rows and time-series charts are all worth more width.

> **v1.86 amendments**: **a host firewall is the one thing that breaks a node invisibly, and the resolver must answer where it says it can** (§7.1, §16.2, §5.2.5, §21). Found on a fresh Debian VPS whose workloads could resolve nothing: ufw, with `deny (incoming)` and `deny (routed)`, and allow rules written for a previous k3s install whose CIDRs are not Kanea's. Two drops were stacked and neither was visible. An alloc's query to the internal resolver is a **new inbound connection to the host on a veth**, so it traverses the *input* hook, and egress traverses *forward*; §5.2.5's standing position is that a foreign drop policy is a finding rather than something the datapath fights, but `doctor` only ever looked at **forward**, so the half that actually broke DNS was never named. The finding is now one **host firewall** check over both hooks, and it has a second half that matters more than the first: a drop policy alone cannot distinguish a correctly configured firewall from a broken one, since both read `policy drop`, so the check also looks for an accept rule naming the cluster CIDR as a source. That makes it **self-clearing** — it passes the moment the rules exist — rather than a warning every firewalled node carries forever, which is the failure mode a check nobody can satisfy always becomes. What `doctor` cannot do is say what to type, because the answer depends on this node's CIDRs, its resolver address and which manager owns the ruleset; **`kanea firewall`** (§16.2) prints exactly that, for ufw, firewalld, nft or iptables, derived from the same values `doctor` checks. It **never applies anything**, and the reason is the same one that keeps the datapath out of foreign tables: a rule Kanea writes into someone else's ruleset is flushed away by its owner on the next reload, so writing one would be a fix that silently stops being applied. Two smaller corrections ride along. `doctor` conflated **"this check failed" with "this user may not run this check"**: a permission error on the containerd socket rendered as *"exists but refuses connections"* and sent an operator to `kanea install` on a healthy node, so results gain a third outcome, **skipped**, which is reported and never counted as a failure. And the internal resolver **sets the TC bit while binding UDP only**: three separate paths tell a client to retry over TCP — an internal answer over 512 bytes, an upstream reply that filled the read buffer, and an upstream reply that already carried TC, which no buffer size can close — and every one of them pointed at an address with nothing listening. It now serves TCP beside UDP as RFC 7766 requires, node-local and bounded the way the UDP path is: a hard concurrent-connection cap whose refusals are counted, deadlines on every connection, and a TCP query forwarded over TCP so the retry can actually carry the answer the first attempt could not.

> **v1.85 amendments**: **a spec can share an environment and can put a file in a container** (§6.2 **R34**/**R35**, §6.1, §21, §14 A03/A05). Two things the job spec could not say, and they are asked for together. **Env groups (R34).** R2's variables substitute `${name}` into text you write, so putting `LOG_LEVEL` into ten services still means writing the key ten times; there is no way to declare a set of environment variables once and have a service *take* it. A top-level `env_group "<name>" { }` names one, and a service opts in with `env_from = ["common", "db"]`. Three properties decide the shape. It is **opt-in per service**, not per project: a project-wide default would mean editing one line rolls every service in the project, and `Env` is SpecHash material, so the blast radius must be something the spec states rather than something it inherits. Groups are **evaluated per consuming service, not once**, because a value may carry `${service.db.host}` and the service reference namespace is project-scoped — one group used from two projects therefore resolves to two different addresses, which is right, and the R9/R10 **dependency edge lands on the consuming service**, which is the property that matters, since a service taking a group that names `postgres` must be ordered behind `postgres`. And **R5 is checked per consumer**: a group is top-level and knows no project, so `secret:shop/x` inside one is validated against *every service that takes the group*, the v1.72 `storage.auth_ref` rule applied to a second top-level block. Precedence is the R20/R30 shape: groups in declaration order, then the container's own `env` on top. Merging happens **at parse**, into the service's env, so the record shape, the hash, the apply seam and every reader are untouched, and changing a group rolls exactly the services that take it. **Mounted files (R35).** Nothing in the spec could put literal content on a node: the only two mechanisms that land a generated file in a container are the R3 secrets tmpfs and `resolv.conf`, and neither takes its bytes from a spec, so the documented answers were "bake it into the image", "mount a `host` volume and place the file yourself", or run a step that writes it into a shared volume. A `file "<name>" { path, mode, content | source }` block declares one. `path` is absolute, clean and `..`-free, refused on collision with a volume or socket mount path and against the same system-path list volumes get; the bind is read-only and carries `nosuid,noexec,nodev`; an `x` bit in `mode` is refused, because a `file` block delivers configuration rather than a program. Files mount **after volumes**, so a file at a path inside a volume wins — otherwise "declare a file at a path" would not mean what it says. **`source` is read by a caller-supplied reader, never by ambient filesystem access, and that is a security rule rather than a plumbing choice**: a spec is parsed in three places and only one of them has a directory. The GitOps sync parses blobs straight out of the commit tree with no working tree at all, and the dashboard's spec editor parses an in-memory string **inside `kanead`, as root** — so a parser that called `os.ReadFile` would make `POST /v1/spec/render` an arbitrary file read as root for any signed-in user. The CLI supplies a reader rooted at the spec's own directory, the syncer one over the commit tree (where a lookup cannot escape the repository and a symlink is refused *by mode*, which is stronger than the v1.75 defence), and the API and MCP supply none, so `source` is refused by name there. **A secret may be interpolated into content, and the value never enters the Store.** `${secret.<scope>.<name>}` resolves at parse to an opaque placeholder carrying a per-parse random nonce and an index into a validated reference list; the record holds the placeholder and the references, exactly as R3 holds a reference for an env var, and the reconciler substitutes at alloc create, so a rotation lands at the next replacement and a restart re-resolves. The nonce is what makes the placeholder unforgeable against arbitrary file content, `\x00` is refused in content so no author can express one at all, and an index can only ever select a reference R5 already scoped. A file carrying any reference is materialised into a **tmpfs of its own** (0400, owned by the reading container) rather than the secrets tmpfs, because that one is 4 MiB shared by every alloc on the node and a config file filling it would fail credential delivery for a service that declares no files; a file carrying none is a shared, world-readable inode on disk, `resolv.conf`'s reasoning applied to config. **Content is SpecHash material**, deliberately and unlike R31's `size` or R33's pull policy: the bytes are placed in the container at create and nothing re-renders them for a running one, so editing a config file rolling the service *is* the feature — with the caveat that the hash canonicalises the nonce away, or an unchanged spec would roll every file-bearing service on every apply. And §21 gains the first byte budgets the platform has ever had for a record: **64 KiB per file, 128 KiB per service, 512 KiB per apply**, plus post-substitution ceilings, because a `Desired` record is CDC-replicated and the change log holds a second full copy of every value it ships. `secret` joins `service` as a name a spec may not declare.
> **v1.84 amendments**: **a service may run setup before it runs, and a spec may say where an image comes from** (§6.2 **R32**/**R33**, §5.2.4, §15.1, §17, §19.3). Two gaps, one of them older than the spec. **Init containers (R32).** There has never been a way to run work *before* a workload starts: a schema migration, a `chown` of a directory §8 just created, a config render. The choices were baking it into the image's entrypoint, where it re-runs on every restart and its failure is indistinguishable from an application crash, or a second service that `depends_on` orders but never finishes, because R10 orders *services* and has no run-to-completion concept. §19.3 parks **multi-task services (sidecars)** and this unparks exactly half of it, deliberately: init containers are *sequential*, so exactly one container of an alloc runs at a time and the feature needs no concurrency story, no shared-PID decision and no second health model. Concurrent sidecars stay parked. An `init "<name>"` block declares its own `image`, `command`, `env`, `resources`, `user`, `capabilities`, `registry_auth_ref`, `pull_policy` and `timeout`, and **shares the alloc**: the same network namespace (so `${service.*}` and internal DNS resolve exactly as they do for the task, which is what makes a wait-for-database step possible), the same volumes at the same mount paths, and the same per-alloc secrets tmpfs. Nothing is inherited from `task`; an omitted `resources` is R11's unbounded and an omitted `user` is R23's "the image's own `USER` stands". The block has **no** `device`, `socket`, `health_check`, `expose`, `scaling`, `count`, `depends_on`, `network` or `build` field, and the absence is the refusal (R25's pattern): an init container is a step, not a service, and delegating R17/R18 grants to one would widen the grant surface for no stated need. **The reconcile loop never waits.** `create` runs inline on the loop's goroutine, so blocking on a five-minute migration would stall every other service on the node; init progress therefore advances one step per pass, in the `Observe`/`Plan`/`apply` shape the loop already has, and the narrowed driver interface gains **no** method — a `Wait` here would be the stall wearing an abstraction's clothes. Three consequences are load-bearing and stated rather than discovered. **The node is the truth and the record is a clock**: completed init containers are kept, stopped, as the evidence that their step ran, so the planner derives the live step from what exists and a lost record write cannot re-run a finished migration; what the record carries is `init_started_at`, because a timeout measured from the daemon's start would silently reset on every `kanead` restart. **A half-run sequence is abandoned, never resumed**, and a sequence re-runs from step 0 on every alloc creation — deploy, crash-restart, spec change — so **init containers must be idempotent**, which is the honest analogue of the netns, the secrets tree and the staged host binds being rebuilt too. And **the failure taxonomy splits where the existing one already splits**: an init container that could not be pulled or created is `image_failed`/`create_failed`/`start_failed`, recorded and retried per pass without spending R29's budget, because nothing ran and a registry outage is on the node's side of the line; one that **ran and exited non-zero** (`init_failed`) or **outlived its `timeout`** (`init_timeout`) is a workload that failed, so it spends the budget and a broken migration stops hammering the database after `attempts`. *Which* step failed goes in the message and in the record's `init_step`/`init_name`, never into the reason vocabulary and never into a new §11 event: `service.crashed` keeps its name, the v1.68 rule verbatim, so no existing filter stops matching. The `timeout` is a **floor, not a deadline** (the kill lands within one reconcile interval) and an absent one is no timeout at all, R11's rule again — an unbounded init blocks nothing, since the pass returns and the alloc sits visibly in the new `init` state with its step and elapsed time. **Pull policy (R33).** `task` and each `init` block may declare `pull_policy`: `if-not-present` (today's behaviour and the default), `never`, or `always`. `never` is the one that was simply missing: an air-gapped or image-preloaded node had no way to say "do not reach for the network", and the resulting pull attempt failed slowly with a message about a registry rather than about the node's own policy. **`always` lowers, at parse time, to R19 auto-update**, and that is the amendment's second real decision: "re-pull on every alloc create" would let two replicas of one spec hash run different bytes, which is the model *a deploy is a spec-hash mismatch* exists to refuse, whereas resolving the tag and pinning the digest rolls **every** replica through `max_parallel`/`min_healthy`/the health check, which is what anybody asking for `always` actually wants. One mechanism, one Store shape, one watcher; `kanea plan` shows the lowered form, and R19's existing refusals (beside a `build` block, without a tag, below the minimum interval) apply, naming `pull_policy` when that is what was written. `always` is refused on an `init` block by name: there is one pinned-image field and it belongs to the task. **Neither `pull_policy` nor `timeout` is SpecHash material** — where an image may be fetched from and how long a step may take are not baked into a container, and hashing either would roll a service to change a policy; the same call made for `Expose`, `Publish`, trigger config and R31's budget, and it needs the `hashableVolumes` treatment because an init block is hashed whole. §15.1 gains an **`images { pull_policy = … }`** stanza as the node-level default, load-once like the rest of the file and under v1.51's precedence verbatim (an explicit `--image-pull-policy` wins and says so, then the stanza, then `if-not-present`); an empty policy on a record means "the node decides", the `Expose.TLSMode` shape, because `toDesired` runs client-side and resolving it there would make one spec mean different things on two machines. Finally, this amendment **adds no spec field for a command override**: R12's `task.command` has been the argument-array entrypoint override since v1.0 and needed nothing; `init` blocks simply get the same field under the same rule.

> **v1.83 amendments**: **an apply may prune, when the request says it is authoritative** (§16.1, §16.2, §11). Removing a `service` block from a spec and re-applying left the service running: §16.1 has made apply additive since v1 (*"nothing absent from the request is ever pruned"*) and that default does not move, because `kanea run` on one file must still not delete what another declares. What changes is that a request can now **state its own scope**: `ApplyRequest.PruneProjects` names the projects it speaks for, and a stored service in one of them that the request does not declare is deleted in the same atomic batch as the puts, so a rename never exists in a state where both or neither service is declared. **The scope is stated rather than inferred, and that is the whole design.** Selectors (v1.57) filter the desired state *before* it is sent, so a server deriving authority from the services present could not distinguish `kanea run app.hcl shop/web` from a spec declaring exactly that service — and would read the first as "delete everything else in shop". `kanea run --remove-orphans` therefore **refuses to combine with a selector**, and with `--image`, which declares no project block and so can never be authoritative for one; both refusals name the alternative, following the doctrine `--image`'s clobber check already set: refuse where the alternative is a silent deletion. Scope is the spec's **`project` blocks** rather than the projects of its services, so a spec can say "this project is now empty" as its last service is removed, which is also why the empty-`Services` refusal is relaxed when a scope is present. `kanea plan --remove-orphans` computes the same scope the same way and renders `- destroy <key>` beside the existing `+ create` / `~ update`; `reconciler.Diff` grows the line behind `DiffScoped` so the callers that cannot prune (MCP's `plan_spec`, the dashboard's spec editor) see exactly what they saw before, a destroy line shown where no prune will happen being worse than none. What a prune destroys is bounded and worth stating: containers, alloc records, the VIP, routes and mounts go; **volume data does not**, so a mistaken prune is recoverable by re-applying the service, and a deliberate one frees no disk. §11 gains **`service.removed`** (warning severity), which closes an older silence rather than only serving this feature: nothing announced a service deletion before, from any path. GitOps is deliberately untouched: a synced repo still applies additively, and pruning there has a prerequisite, since `storeApplier` writes to the Store directly rather than through the apply seam.

> **v1.82 amendments**: **the CLI can talk to a node it is not sitting on, and `kanea deploy` is the verb a pipeline needs** (§16.2, §13.1). `api.NewClient` took a filesystem path and dialled a unix socket unconditionally, so `kanea` was usable only by someone on the node; a GitLab or GitHub job that had just built an image had no way to say so except `ssh`, hand-rolled `curl`, or committing a digest into a spec repository and waiting for the git webhook. The daemon needed nothing for this: `identify` has checked `Authorization: Bearer` before the socket shortcut since §13.2 and `checkCSRF` has always exempted token callers, so **what was missing was a client, not a permission**. `KANEA_URL`, `KANEA_TOKEN` and `KANEA_CA_CERT` (with `--url`/`--token`/`--ca-cert` for parity) select a remote endpoint; environment first, because that is the interface a pipeline actually has. Nothing about authorization moves: the token's role decides exactly what it decided over HTTP, so a viewer still cannot deploy and the CLI is never more privileged than the credential it presents. Four refusals are part of the feature rather than incidental: a token with no endpoint, a CA with no endpoint, **`http://` beyond loopback** (a bearer token in clear text — the rule `listenNetwork` already applies to the daemon's own listener, shared now as `IsPublicHost` so the two ends cannot disagree about what loopback means), and **a URL with no token**, because over a network every authenticated route answers 401 and the usual cause is a CI secret that was never exported, which arrives as the empty string; an empty variable is therefore treated as unset. `--ca-cert` accepts a path *or* the PEM itself, since a CI secret is handed to a job as a value and requiring a file first is the step people skip by reaching for a skip-verify flag — which is why **there is no skip-verify flag**, recorded under *Deliberately not built*. `kanea upgrade` and `kanea mcp` refuse a remote endpoint by name: the first restarts this machine's units and overwrites this machine's binary, and the second's credential *is* the local socket (§16.3 already gives remote agents the node's own `/mcp`). **`kanea deploy <project/service> <image>`** is the deploy verb: it reads the stored record, changes one field and writes the whole thing back, because there is no route that sets an image and round-tripping is what stops a deploy silently dropping a field the command does not know about — the recipe MCP's `deploy_service` already used, now shared rather than duplicated. It waits for convergence by default, so a pipeline goes red when the image does not come up. And **`kanea run --image` now refuses to clobber**: it builds a `Desired` from nothing, so over a service carrying ports, expose, env, volumes, a health check or scaling it deleted the difference silently. It refuses **on loss, not on existence**, naming what would go and pointing at `kanea deploy`, so the §6.2 R8 image-only path stays first-class and re-running the documented quickstart on a bare service is still idempotent.

> **v1.81 amendments**: **the internal resolver forwards where the host forwards, loopback included** (§5.2.5, §15.1). `HostResolvers` dropped every loopback nameserver out of `/etc/resolv.conf`, on the stated grounds that an alloc cannot reach one. That named the wrong reader, and the mistake was total rather than partial: **an alloc never receives these addresses at all**. Its `/etc/resolv.conf` is written by `WriteResolvConf` and carries the internal resolver and a search domain, nothing else; the forwarding list has exactly one consumer, `kanead`'s own forwarder, which dials it with a plain `net.Dialer` from `kanead`'s network namespace — so `127.0.0.53` is precisely as reachable there as it is for every other process on the node. The cost of the error was not theoretical: a stock Debian or Ubuntu server runs systemd-resolved, whose stub is the *only* nameserver in `/etc/resolv.conf`, so the drop left an empty list and the daemon **refused to start**, in a crash loop, on the most ordinary DNS configuration there is. It was found on a fresh VPS, which is the v1.53 genre: invisible in development, where a workstation's resolv.conf names a real address, and fatal on the first cloud node to run `kanea init`. Nothing about the refusal was recoverable from the outside either — `kanea init` writes no `kanea.hcl` by design (§15.1's default is that the file does not exist), it has no flag that would render `--dns-upstream` into the unit, and no preflight check named the condition, so the documented install path produced a unit that could not boot. Forwarding to the stub is also the better answer on its own merits, not merely the tolerable one: it is the host's resolver, so a workload inherits the node's cache, its DNSSEC posture and any split-horizon view a link supplies, which is what "the host's own resolvers" was always supposed to mean. Precedence is untouched (`--dns-upstream` > the `dns` stanza > `/etc/resolv.conf`, still read once at startup), and the empty-file refusal stays: a resolv.conf naming no nameserver at all is still an error, because there is nothing to forward to. **One hazard the loopback ban had been covering by accident is now covered on purpose**: an upstream that *is* this resolver — a loopback bind beside a loopback nameserver, or a node whose resolv.conf was pointed at Kanea's resolver deliberately — makes every external query loop until the forward timeout and return SERVFAIL, which reads as a broken internet rather than a misconfiguration. `NewDNS` drops such an entry by name and refuses only when it was the sole upstream; addresses are compared parsed rather than as text, because `[::1]:53` and `[0:0:0:0:0:0:0:1]:53` are one resolver spelled two ways.

> **v1.80 amendments**: **init provisions the listener's certificate itself, and the listen flow converges** (§16.2, §15.1, §13.1, §14 A05). Since v1.45 the prompt refused a beyond-loopback answer with no certificate pair, and since v1.61 the escapes existed — the `bind` stanza's four `api_tls` modes — but only for an operator who already knew the file; everyone else met a dead stop naming `--listen-cert/--listen-key` and loopback, on exactly the fresh VPS where no certificate exists yet. §13.1's rule is *TLS beyond loopback*, and the dead stop existed only because init had nothing to satisfy it with — so init now satisfies it: a public address at the prompt or in a bare `--listen` (no explicit pair, no owning `bind` stanza) **provisions a default certificate**, a static ten-year single-host self-signed pair at `/etc/kanea/api.crt` + `api.key` (0600), an IP-shaped host getting an IP SAN (the v1.61 rule), rendered into the unit as `--listen-cert/--listen-key` and served as `provided`. **The override chain is unchanged and now reads as precedence**: an explicit pair wins at the flag layer; a `bind` stanza owns the listener whenever it exists (no explicit `--listen`), and init renders no listen flags at all, exactly as v1.61 wrote it; the provisioned default covers only what neither claimed. The pair is minted once and **never re-minted** — re-minting would flip the fingerprint operators have already accepted, the master key's "never regenerated" rule at a smaller stake — and half a pair is an error rather than a guess. An unspecified host (`0.0.0.0`, `:8600`) is the one refusal left: a SAN needs something to name, `validateBind`'s rule since v1.61, and the refusal says bind a specific address or set `bind.api_domain`. **The restart half converges**: init restarts kanead whenever the settled listener differs from the daemon's *configured* address (`health.Listen` is reported verbatim, so an unchanged re-run compares equal and nothing restarts) — before this, a re-run that moved the listener wrote the new unit and left the old process serving the old address, and a re-run that retired the listener with `none` never closed it.

> **v1.79 amendments**: **the charts have their data when they open** (§9.1, §12.1, §12.2, §16.1). The dashboard seeded its sparklines with a separate `GET /v1/stats/history` per panel while its live numbers came over the socket, so a fresh page drew nothing until a REST round trip landed, and a navigation away and back paid for it again. Three absences made it worse: the per-alloc sparklines had **no seedable route at all** (the ranged read serves service-level subjects, the rings key allocs as `project/service/<alloc>`), the Overview's node tiles had no recorded series to seed from, and six series the pipeline records (`p50`, `p99`, `error_rate`, `flows_per_second`, `drops_per_second`, `memory_bytes`) were exposed by no ranged read. Meanwhile the Overview polled `GET /v1/stats` every ten seconds over REST beside a socket it was already holding. **The stats subscription now carries its own seed**: a `history` request field asks for it, it rides the *first* frame of the subscription and no frame after it (so a snapshot frame is still a superset of the one it supersedes, §12.1), and it is absent unless asked for, so every pre-v1.79 client's frames are byte-identical. It is **opt-in rather than always-on** because the services page subscribes stats per table row: a seed nobody asked for, twenty times over, is the v1.70 defect wearing a new hat. A **series selector** is what makes everything seedable without making the default frame bigger; the default sets are exactly the ones v1.38 and v1.42 shipped, and an unknown name is refused by name rather than silently dropped, because a chart that seeds nothing must not look like a service with no traffic. A **node topic** replaces the Overview's REST poll with pushes on the scrape interval and its own seed. Bounds, all stated rather than discovered later: one seed frame is capped by total points, the per-alloc half is **dropped whole rather than truncated to a second window** (two windows in one frame is a client rebuilding slots against the wrong interval, which draws a *wrong* chart rather than no chart), and a per-session seed budget answers resubscribe amplification, a ~100-byte request against a ~130 KiB answer. Two node series are added under exporter-invisible names, `node_load1` and `node_allocs_running`; the second is counted inside the alloc-map refresh the metrics path **already performs**, so it costs no Store read that was not already happening, which is the condition under which §18 rule 2 permits it at all, and it lands at that refresh's cadence, so most slots are gaps and must never be forward-filled. Node read-time aggregates are memoized per raw slot (`Record` drops out-of-order samples, so a slot's answer is final once the slot has passed), which is what makes N Overview subscribers cost one ring walk instead of N. **Two defects fixed, both of which had been drawing wrong pictures.** `interval_seconds` was decided from the window asked for while `Range` decided the tier from how long ago `from` was - a difference of microseconds that tipped a one-hour window to the 60 s rollup while the response advertised 5 s, after which the client placed every point in the wrong slot and drew a chart twelve times too compressed; the tier decision is now one exported function with two callers, and both surfaces truncate their range to a slot boundary so there is no epsilon left to tip it. And `NodeReader` was one stateful instance whose CPU delta *any* reader consumed, so an inbound `GET /v1/stats` silently made the **next** recorded `node_cpu_percent` cover a fraction of a second: a real reading of the wrong interval, which is worse than a gap because nothing about it looks wrong. The sampler now owns the schedule and every reader answers from the sample it produced. The dashboard's half is stated in §12.2: a chart distinguishes *loading* from *measured nothing*, and the second says **"no samples yet"**, which is also the honest thing to say for the ten seconds after a restart during which no rate can exist.

> **v1.78 amendments**: **the udp relay is not an amplifier** (§7.2.2, `docs/THREAT_MODEL.md` §3.13; audit K-12). One spoofed datagram could open a session whose every backend datagram went to the forged address - a reflector running from the node's IP, refreshed indefinitely by a streaming backend. Two bounds now assume spoofing: a **per-source-IP session cap** (8) beside the listener cap, because the listener cap keys on the full address and a flood varies the port; and a **pre-verification byte cap** (4 KiB) on what a session may send toward its client until a *second* datagram arrives from the same socket - the weak proof of receipt a spoofed source cannot produce, sized to answer a real DNS/WireGuard handshake while making amplification impossible (below the request it answers). Both refusals are counted (`source_limit`, `unverified_cap`). The residual is stated in §3.13: a streaming backend still moves bounded traffic per spoofed session until the table fills, and no stronger proof exists without protocol knowledge the relay does not have.

> **v1.77 amendments**: **source identity is bound to the veth, exactly** (§5.2.5, `docs/THREAT_MODEL.md` §3.5; audit K-09). The v1.65 anti-spoof check verified only that a packet's claimed source was *inside the cluster CIDR*; anything in-cluster passed, so an alloc could forge any cluster source - including the host anchor (`IDENTITY_FLAG_HOST` passed policy before protocol gating) - with `IP_FREEBIND` + `bind()`, no capability needed, and per-alloc addresses are public knowledge (the embedded DNS publishes them). TCP handshakes never completed (replies return to the real owner), but cross-project UDP/ICMP delivery and one-way delivery *as the host* were real, and §3.5's "can learn an address exists, not talk to it" and §4's "no route to another project's services" were both false under forgery. The fix is two new pinned maps beside the others, per the v1.41 ABI rule: `veth_src` / `veth_src6`, keyed by host-veth ifindex, holding the one address kanead assigned that veth; `from_container` drops `DROP_SPOOF` on any mismatch **and on a miss** (fail-closed, the identity-miss rule). The binding is written in `plumb` while the host side is still down, deleted in `Detach`/`teardownPartial`, re-asserted idempotently by `reuseExisting` and `RepairIdentity`, and - the migration half - populated by `Init` for every pre-upgrade veth *before* the refreshed programs attach to it, because a program that fails closed on a miss would otherwise blackhole a running workload at upgrade. The cluster-CIDR maps stay: `to_container` still needs them for the external-return-traffic pass. A stale binding left by a crashed Detach is only ever consulted by our own programs and is overwritten by the next attach on that ifindex, so no sweep exists and none is needed.

> **v1.76 amendments**: **env-secret delivery now matches R3's text: tmpfs files are the default, inline env values are the documented opt-in** (§6.2 R3, §14 A02, `docs/THREAT_MODEL.md` §3.3). The wire semantics are pinned: an env value of `secret:<project>/<name>` is resolved at alloc create into a per-alloc tmpfs directory (`/run/kanea/secrets/<alloc>/<scope>/<name>`, files 0400 owned by the alloc's effective uid/gid, directory tree 0711, bind-mounted read-only at the same path in the container) and the environment variable carries the **file's path**; the new `secret-env:<project>/<name>` form carries the **value inline** for software that only reads environment variables, and is the documented weaker option (visible in `/proc/<pid>/environ` to the alloc's own uid). Both forms scope identically (R5), both parse through `secrets.ParseEnvRef`, and the plan-time/apply-time checks cannot drift because they share it. The Store record always keeps the reference - a resolved value never enters the Store, an API response, a backup, or a log - so rotation lands on the alloc's next replacement and a restart re-resolves. An unresolvable reference fails the alloc (`secrets_failed`), loudly and before any container exists, because starting without the credential the spec declared is the §9.2 mistake in a credential's clothes. The per-alloc tree is rebuilt on every create (stale entries cannot survive a rotation) and removed with the alloc; tmpfs backing means the value never touches disk. Under `--network netns` dev mode the same layout applies with a plain directory instead of tmpfs, since the point of netns is a dev laptop, not a threat boundary.

> **v1.75 amendments**: **the build network position is stated, and the metadata class is closed** (§10.2, §14 A10, `docs/THREAT_MODEL.md` §3.21). A Dockerfile `RUN` step is repo-controlled code executing with **host networking** (`rootlesskit --net=host`, so a node-local registry stays reachable), and the §5.2.5 egress guard - attached to alloc veths - never saw it: anyone who could push to a synced repository could read the cloud instance metadata service from a build step, and nothing in the threat model said so. The section now exists (§3.21: what a build reaches, what it cannot, and the residuals), and the control exists beside it: an output-chain **drop of `169.254.0.0/16` for the `kanea-buildkit` uid** in the datapath's owned nftables table, re-ensured with the masquerade rule (one table, one rewrite - a second writer would flush it away). Rootless uid-mapping puts the daemon and every root `RUN` step on that one uid, so the account is the match; a `USER <non-root>` step runs as a subuid and escapes it, which §3.21 records as a residual alongside loopback diagnostics and cross-project VIP reachability, both awaiting the worker-network-namespace evaluation rather than silently unbuilt. A10's row is updated to name the second enforcement point, because "the metadata range never leaves an alloc" was only ever half the sentence.

> **v1.74 amendments**: **the backup manifest is authenticated, and a restore's segment chain must be a chain** (§15.3). The archive format's own audit found the gap the "AEAD-authenticated and hashed" claim hid: the snapshot's hash is recorded *in the manifest*, and the manifest was an unauthenticated JSON document - so a bucket-write attacker (the adversary §15.3 declares in scope: "the bucket is assumed hostile") could substitute an older, entirely genuine (manifest, snapshot) pair, and every check would pass because both halves were produced by the key. A restore would roll the node back past revocations: deleted accounts, revoked tokens and removed secrets resurrect, and the reconciler starts whatever a delete in the gap had removed. Two rules close it. **The manifest is MAC'd**: a third HKDF key (`"kanea backup manifest mac v1"`, the stream key's sibling) HMAC-SHA256s the canonical manifest bytes; `Fetch`/`Restore` verify it before trusting any field, `KeyID` included - the old `KeyID`-empty skip let an attacker strip the fingerprint, and the fingerprint now rides under the MAC like everything else. A keyed MAC is not a signature and claims nothing about attribution: the deliberately-not-built decision stands, because a bucket several nodes write to still does not exist. **Pre-v1.74 archives restore with a loud warning**, never silently: operator-initiated, and the warning says to verify the archive id and created-at are the state meant. **Replay refuses a non-contiguous chain**: the first segment above the snapshot must start where the snapshot ends, and each segment must start at its predecessor's inclusive end plus one. The unreadable-segment rule already stopped at damage; a segment *deleted from the bucket* was invisible to it, and §15.3's own sentence says what skipping one does ("the record it removed would come back from the dead"). The refusal carries the same honest escape hatch: `--skip-replay` accepts the snapshot alone. §15.3's parenthetical "SHA-256 manifest verified before restore" is updated to match.

> **v1.73 amendments**: **the git source and the build block get the validation the rest of the schema has** (§10.1, §10.2). Two gaps sat open for any record that reached the node, GitOps-pushed specs included. **`git.url` now has a shape**: `https://`, `ssh://`, scp-form, or a local path - `http://` is refused by name (the deploy token travels as a BasicAuth password on every fetch, and over cleartext that is the credential), `git://` is refused beside it (unauthenticated plaintext, so anyone on the path serves the content the sync then builds and deploys), and **userinfo is refused** in any form (a credential in the URL lands in build logs and sync errors any viewer can read, which is the escape `auth_ref` exists to prevent; `ssh://git@host/…` keeps its login *name*, but a password there is refused like any other). The URL is additionally redacted at every log and error call site, for records that predate the refusal. **`build.target`/`build.cache_repo` must parse as image references and `build.tag` may not contain a comma, an `=` or whitespace**: buildctl parses `--output`, `--export-cache` and `--import-cache` as comma-separated `key=value` lists, so `tag = "x,registry.insecure=true"` was not a name at all - it was a second exporter attribute silently downgrading the push to cleartext. Both rules are checked twice, in the codebase's shape: at `plan` for whoever wrote the spec, and in the runner after `ExpandTag` for records (and branch names, which `${GIT_BRANCH}` sanitizes for slashes, not for option syntax) that never meet the parser. And two path rules land with them, from the same review: `build.context`/`build.dockerfile` refuse absolute and `..` forms at plan (the runner already refuses them, and now refuses symlinks in the context path component-by-component - a checkout writes symlink blobs verbatim and buildkit's context reader resolves the root through `EvalSymlinks`, as root).

> **v1.72 amendments**: **storage credentials join R5, and storage endpoints join the https rule** (§8). The `storage` block's `auth_ref` was the one credential reference in the schema that no validator scoped: git, registry, notification and auth references have been R5-checked at plan since they existed, but a storage block is top-level, nothing checked it, and mount-time resolution deliberately knows no project - so a spec reaching the node through any channel (a synced repository being the one §10.1 arms) could mount *another project's* S3 or SMB credential, and a hostile `endpoint` received that credential on every request. Two rules close it, both at plan where an operator should meet them. **`auth_ref` is validated against every service that mounts the storage**: mounting *is* reading, so the reference must name each mounting service's own project or `shared/` (R5's vocabulary, unchanged); a literal value is refused by shape exactly as it is everywhere else. **`endpoint` must be `https://`**: the mount helper sends the resolved credential to that address on every request - SigV4 signs, and the access key travels in the `Authorization` header - so a cleartext endpoint is the credential, which is the sentence `validateNotifyURL` has said since M8. The §8 example's `auth_ref = "secret:storage/s3-media"` is corrected to `secret:shop/s3-media` under the rule it now illustrates, the v1.22 maneuver repeated one section down.

> **v1.71 amendments**: **the dashboard's last two pages, and the two verbs they do not have** (§12.2, §16.1). §12.2 has listed a **Projects** page and a **Storage** page since v1.1 and neither existed: projects appeared only as a filter dropdown on other pages, and volumes (routed, sampled and CLI-visible since v1.69) had no surface in the browser at all, so the one place an operator watches a disk fill up was a terminal. Both are built, and the shape of each is decided by what the daemon actually holds. **Projects** leads with git sync status, because for a synced project "is what I pushed what is running" is the question, and its **Sync now** action is the poll loop's verb, never the webhook's: it marks the project and lets the sync loop re-read the source over Kanea's own credential (§10.1). It has **no create and no delete**, and that is the amendment: §12.2's "create/edit" described verbs `internal/api` deliberately does not have, because a project is the namespace a service declares itself into (§4.2) and a second way to make one would be a second source of truth about which projects exist. "New project" is a service deployed into a new name (the page says so and links to the spec editor) and the last service leaving takes the project with it. §12.2's "description" column goes the same way: a `project`/`service` description is parsed and validated (≤ 512 chars) but **never stored** (`Desired` has no such field) so the page cannot show one, and adding it is a Store-shape decision with R23's hashing rules attached, not a dashboard change. **Storage** renders `GET /v1/volumes` as it is derived (v1.69): storage resources with their mounts nested underneath, driver and target, measured usage against a declared **budget** that is measured and never enforced (R31), and mount state. Two of that route's honest limits are on the page rather than behind it: a resource **nothing references does not appear**, and an unmeasured volume is a dash rather than a zero (§9.2), permanently so for `s3`, which is never walked. Neither page takes a sidebar badge: each would cost the shell a poll of its own on every page, and neither number is one anybody waits for the way a running build or a new alert is. §16.1 is corrected to match on the two families this touches: `POST`/`DELETE /v1/projects` and `GET|POST /v1/storage` were never built and should not be; the project routes as they exist (list, detail, sync, per-project notifications) and `GET /v1/volumes` replace them.

> **v1.70 amendments**: **a log stream stops costing the socket it rides on** (§12.1, §17). The live WebSocket emitted **one frame per log line** into a 64-frame send buffer whose overflow policy was to close the connection, and the dashboard subscribes with a 200-line tail, so the log panel killed the socket the instant it mounted, on any service with more than sixty-odd lines of history. Because §12.1 mandates *one* multiplexed socket, `services`, `allocs` and `stats` died with it, the client reconnected, replayed the same subscription and died again: a permanent reconnect loop presenting as a dashboard that never goes live. Observed on a real node, where it closed in 20 ms with three frames delivered. Three changes, and each is a separate fault. **The log feed is batched**: one frame per poll tick carrying every line of that tick, so the frame count is bounded by the tick rate rather than by how much a workload writes. **Batches are capped** (per line count and per byte total) because batching otherwise converts an unbounded frame *count* into an unbounded frame *size*, and the excess is **dropped oldest-first and counted in the frame itself**, never held: a held backlog is the unbounded daemon-side queue §17's drop policy exists to forbid, and it makes a "live" tail silently minutes stale. The client-supplied `tail` is **clamped on the node**, before the tailer is built, because satisfying it means reading the file backwards. And **the socket's overflow policy becomes per topic**: a data frame on a lossy topic is dropped and counted while the connection lives; every other topic, and every error frame, still closes as before; a snapshot frame supersedes the last one, so dropping it would leave the client believing stale data was current. What that gives up is stated plainly: the buffer was doing double duty as the defence against a peer that never reads, and that defence is now the 30 s ping under a 10 s write timeout, which is what actually catches a dead peer; the buffer only ever caught the *live* one that fell behind in a burst, which is the dashboard. One accepted consequence: a browser tab left open across an upgrade shows an empty log panel until it is reloaded, its old schema rejecting the batched shape; every other topic on that tab keeps working. Separately, the log feed now **re-selects its allocs** instead of choosing them once at subscribe and giving up when none has written a file yet: a subscription that outlived a deploy, restart or scale-up streamed nothing and said nothing, which is the same silence in a different place.

> **v1.69 amendments**: **volumes become visible, and say something about themselves** (§8, §6.2 R15/R31, §11, §16.2). Volumes were the least observable thing on a node: no `/v1/volumes` route and no `kanea volume` command existed, so the only way to see what was mounted was `kanea describe` on each service in turn, and when a disk filled up there was nothing to look at. Three additions, and the shape of each is decided by what the platform can honestly deliver. **`kanea volume list` / `GET /v1/volumes`** is a derived view (storage resources with their mounts nested underneath) computed by grouping the volume records services already carry. It is deliberately *not* backed by a new Store kind, which has one honest consequence stated here rather than discovered later: a storage block that **nothing references cannot appear**, because `toDesired` inlines each resource into the volumes that use it and nothing on the node remembers the rest (the same fact that makes §16.2's generator refuse a spec with volume blocks). **`create = true` on a `host` storage block** (R15) makes Kanea create the directory, and R15's default does not move: absent the flag, a host path that does not exist is still refused, because "creating it on demand turns a typo into a volume that is silently empty" remains true for everyone who did not ask. The allowlist is checked against the nearest **existing** ancestor *before* anything is created, so a `create` outside a permitted prefix never makes a directory, and the full ordinary check runs again afterwards, one code path decides whether a directory may be mounted. **`size` on a `volume` block** (R31) is a **budget, not a quota**: Kanea measures the volume's real usage against it and reports and notifies, and claims nothing more. That is the honest shape available: no filesystem quota mechanism exists on the node, `nfs`/`smb`/`s3` cannot carry one at all, and a `size` that silently enforced nothing would be R21's dropped control. Usage is sampled by a background walk on a slow interval, timeout-bounded through the same abandonable-goroutine discipline §8's mount code already follows (M0 spike ③), **in memory only** (constraint #2) and **skipped by name for `s3`**, where a FUSE walk is a LIST per directory. Unmeasured, timed-out, skipped and undeclared all render **absent, never zero** (§9.2). Size lives on the `volume` block rather than `storage` for R24's reason exactly; two services mounting one resource get distinct targets, so the budget is the volume's decision, and neither `size` nor `create` is SpecHash material: nothing about a budget or a `mkdir` is baked into a container, and rolling a database to change a monitoring threshold would be the wrong behaviour (the `Expose`/`Publish`/trigger-config rule). §11 gains the **`volume.*` family**: `over_budget`/`under_budget` and `mount_failed`/`mount_recovered`, in recovery pairs like `service.unhealthy`/`healthy`, because an alert that never clears trains people to ignore it. The mount half closes a real gap: the supervisor has always remounted a failed mount and counted it, and **never told anyone**. Every one of the four fires on a **transition**, never per sample or per probe: a breached budget persists for hours and a mount is probed every 30 s, so an event per observation would be a metric wearing an event's name. Purely additive to the vocabulary, so no existing filter changes meaning.

> **v1.68 amendments**: **an alloc says why it died** (§9.3, §5.2.11, §6.2 R11, §11, §17). Until now the whole durable record of a termination was one integer, `LastExitCode`, and every human-facing explanation was a format string re-derived from it at the point of display, so an **OOM kill was indistinguishable from any other `SIGKILL`**: both are exit 137, and so is `kanea stop`. Nothing read the OOM fact anywhere; §5.2.11 and R11 have promised "breach OOM-kills the alloc, event emitted" since v1.4 and the event never carried the cause. This closes that, and the closure matters more since **v1.58 made an omitted `resources` block mean unbounded**: an OOM now usually comes from the *collective* ceiling (§5.2.11) rather than a declared per-alloc limit, and the two want opposite fixes; raise the service's limit, or stop overcommitting the node. The record gains a **termination reason** and a one-line message: `oom_killed`, `signal` (naming the signal), `error`, `completed`, and (because an alloc that never started is the same question wearing different clothes) the six start-failure reasons `image_failed`, `volume_failed`, `passthrough_failed`, `network_failed`, `create_failed`, `start_failed`, one per failure point on the create path. **The OOM fact is read, never inferred**: the alloc cgroup's `memory.events` `oom_kill` counter is consulted when a stopped alloc is observed with a non-zero exit, and an unreadable or already-reaped cgroup means the exit is classified `signal`, never OOM; §9.2's "no data is never zero" applied to a cause, and the reason `kanea stop`'s own 137 cannot be mistaken for a memory kill. When the counter fired and the alloc declared **no** limit, the message names the collective ceiling rather than a number nobody typed. Deliberately **not** changed: the §11 vocabulary; `service.crashed` carries the reason in its message and keeps its name, because an existing `on = ["service.crashed"]` filter that silently stopped matching OOM crashes is precisely the failure the vocabulary is centralized to prevent; start-failure **retry semantics**, which stay per-pass and never touch R29's restart budget (the budget is for crashes); and `kanea ps`, which is a list. The reason is a **detail-view fact** and surfaces in `kanea describe`, the dashboard's service detail, and the API: where it costs no new projection, `AllocRecord` being the wire format directly.

> **v1.67 amendments**: **the Homebrew formula moves to its own tap repository** (§5.2.12), superseding v1.60's "the formula lives in this repository". The formula, its generator (`update-formula.sh`) and the CI that verifies it now live in **`m18h/homebrew-kanea`**: Homebrew's tap convention, so `brew tap m18h/kanea` resolves it with no explicit URL. What v1.60 actually rejected was the *cross-repo credential*, not the second repository, and the move is built so no such credential comes into existence: **the tap repository updates itself**; a scheduled workflow polls `m18h/kanea`'s latest release over the public API, downloads its `checksums.txt`, regenerates the formula with the same generator, and commits, so this repository's release workflow keeps writing only to this repository and there is nothing beyond a repository's own `GITHUB_TOKEN` to rot. The cost is a bump lag of up to the poll interval (one hour), accepted for an optional CLI channel; the formula's `livecheck` (`github_latest`) reports the new version in the meantime. Everything else in v1.60 stands: the darwin archives remain published by this repository's release workflow (the formula's URLs name them), the formula remains generated output; hand-editing it is drift, not a contribution, and the tap repository's CI regenerates and diffs to say so by name; the generator still reads asset names out of `checksums.txt`, so the release.yml/install.sh/`assetName` naming contract keeps its reader, now in the tap repository. Homebrew remains a CLI channel, never a node channel, and the formula's CI still style-checks, install-tests and runs the formula test on both platforms, in the tap repository's CI rather than this repository's.

> **v1.66 amendments**: **the `dns` stanza: upstream resolvers become node config** (§15.1, §5.2.5). `kanea.hcl` gains its fourth read stanza: `dns { upstreams = ["1.1.1.1", "10.0.0.53:5353"] }` names the resolvers the internal DNS forwards external queries to. The gap it closes is real and was met in the v1.65 investigation: with no `--dns-upstream` flag the daemon reads `/etc/resolv.conf` **once at startup** and holds that list for the process's life, so a node whose DHCP resolver changed (or whose resolv.conf pointed somewhere unfortunate at boot) kept forwarding to it until someone restarted kanead, and the only way to pin better upstreams was a hand-edited unit. Precedence is v1.51's doctrine verbatim: **an explicit `--dns-upstream` wins and the stanza is not consulted** (said in the log when both exist), the stanza otherwise supplies the list, and no stanza means today's behavior; the host's own resolv.conf. Entries are validated at parse with file and line, by the same rule the daemon applies (`normalizeUpstream`'s: an address, or a host:port pair; a bare address gets `:53`); an **empty list is refused by name**; it configures nothing, and a stanza that means "no upstreams" would silently turn external resolution into SERVFAIL, which is R21's dropped control wearing DNS clothes. Still load-once, deliberately: the stanza is how an operator *pins* upstreams, and pinning that reloaded would be a third behavior between the flag and resolv.conf. The listen address stays a flag (`--dns-listen`): where the resolver binds is install-time topology like the CIDRs, not the mutable half this stanza carries. The flag's validation now refuses public addresses by name (v1.78's audit pass): loopback, private and link-local binds only, because a public one is an open resolver and a service inventory on the internet - the two outcomes the check's comment always claimed to prevent.

> **v1.65 amendments**: **return traffic returns: the deny-closed identity check learns where the cluster ends** (§5.2.5, §14 A10, §15.4, `docs/THREAT_MODEL.md` §3.5). Fixes a defect the datapath shipped with in v1.36 and the spike never caught: `kanea_to_container` dropped any packet whose *source* address missed the identity map, **before** the SYN gate, and a reply from the internet, un-NATed by conntrack, has an external source that can never be in a map only kanead's allocator writes. Containers could send but never receive from outside the node; DNS kept working because the resolver answers from the host anchor, which is exactly why the failure read as "DNS resolves, connections hang." The spike's egress check asserted the masquerade *counter*, not reachability: a lesson §20 now owes its checklist. The fix: the deny-closed source check applies to **cluster-internal sources only**. A source inside the cluster CIDR with no identity is an alloc mid-teardown: dropped, exactly as before. A source *outside* it is the world answering an egress connection and passes to a destination that must still hold a local identity; the containment argument is unchanged from §7.2's model: the pod CIDR is private and unroutable from off-node, published ports terminate at the edge, and conntrack un-NATs only established flows, so nothing unsolicited arrives this way unless an operator deliberately routes the cluster CIDR at the node, which is that operator's choice. The cluster prefix reaches the programs through **new maps beside** (`cluster_v4`, `cluster_v6`) (never a widened `dp_config`, the v1.41 ABI rule) with the all-zero birth state reading as "no cluster configured," which drops: a program that outruns its configuration fails closed. Three restart-hardening halves ride along, each closing a gap the investigation confirmed: (1) **tc programs are re-attached to every owned veth at Init**; `FilterReplace` is atomic, so a kanead upgrade delivers new datapath programs node-wide instead of only to allocs created afterwards, and it is what makes shipping this fix reach a running node; (2) **identity entries are repaired, not just observed**: an attachment that exists but is not Ready (the state a pinned-map schema wipe leaves behind) gets its identity re-written by the reconciler through the new repair capability, map-only, never a re-plumb under a running workload; (3) **the masquerade rule and `net.ipv4.ip_forward` are re-ensured periodically** rather than once at startup; a firewall manager that flushes the ruleset mid-flight (firewalld reload, `ufw enable`) previously killed egress silently until the next kanead restart, and v4 forwarding was never set at all, inherited from "the node's existing configuration" (usually: docker's). `kanea doctor` finally grows the network findings v1.36's text promised and never implemented: a foreign FORWARD-drop policy, `ip_forward=0`, and a missing `kanea` nftables table are each a named finding.

> **v1.64 amendments**: **the dashboard reaches exec, and a deploy is visible as data** (§12.1, §12.2, §13.3, §14 A01, §16.1, §16.2). Two additive API changes, both born of the dashboard being a browser. (1) The §13.3 CSRF token gains a **second carrier for WebSocket upgrades**: a browser's `WebSocket` constructor cannot set `X-Kanea-CSRF`, so a cookie-authenticated upgrade to a mutating socket route (`GET /v1/exec` is the only one) may present the token as a `Sec-WebSocket-Protocol` entry `kanea-csrf.<token>` beside the negotiable `kanea.exec.v1`. Same token, same constant-time comparison, same double-submit argument; only the envelope moves, and only where the header cannot exist: the carrier is honored solely on an `Upgrade: websocket` request, a shape no cross-site fetch or form can produce (`Upgrade` and `Sec-*` are browser-forbidden headers), the server echoes only the protocol name and never the token entry, and the §12.1 Origin allowlist still runs before the token is even read. A query-parameter carrier was rejected (a credential in a URL outlives its request in history, devtools and any intermediary) and a one-time exec ticket was rejected as a second credential system solving a solved problem: the cross-site page cannot read the token either way, so a ticket adds no property the token lacks. Browser exec sessions spend the same websocket connection cap a CLI exec does. (2) **`GET /v1/services` and the WS `services` topic serve each service with a computed `spec_hash`**: `reconciler.SpecHash` over the desired record, computed at projection time, never stored, hash material untouched. Beside the `spec_hash` alloc records already carry, a client detects a deploy in flight from any source (apply, restart, GitOps sync, auto-update) by the planner's own staleness rule (a non-empty alloc hash differing from the desired one), instead of a bespoke `deploying` flag that would freeze one interpretation of "in progress" into the server and still need the hash the moment per-alloc progress is wanted.

> **v1.63 amendments**: **shared variables: R2 finally gets its declaration sites** (§6.1, §6.2 R2/R30, §15.1, §16.1). A top-level `variables { }` block in the spec and a `variables { }` stanza in `/etc/kanea/kanea.hcl` both feed the one flat `${VAR}` vocabulary R2 has promised since v1.0, one namespace with the built-ins, because the machinery (`Options.Vars` → the decode's EvalContext) has evaluated every attribute against it all along, and a second `var.`-prefixed vocabulary would be two spellings for one concept. Precedence is defaults-then-specialize, the R20 shape: node stanza under spec block under caller-supplied values (a pipeline's `GIT_*`); the reserved names (the R2 built-ins and `service`) are refused as declarations at every level. **R30** states the rules: values are primitives (string, number, bool; a list or object is refused by name), a variable's value may reference node variables and built-ins but never another spec variable (one rule instead of an ordering-and-cycles story), duplicate declarations across a spec's files are refused, and an undefined reference stays what it has always been; an unknown-variable parse error with file and line. **Variables are not secrets, stated plainly**: they are plaintext in files whose trust model is policy, not confidentiality, and the new authenticated read-only **`GET /v1/vars`** serves the node's stanza to any signed-in caller; a secret stays `secret:<path>` (R3), and a variable whose value *contains* such a reference is fine, because R5 scoping validates the substituted result. Substitution happens at parse, so a changed value is an ordinary spec-hash mismatch rolling through the normal deploy path (nothing new in the SpecHash material) and generation (`GET /v1/spec/source`) emits resolved literals, which the generated-file marker has always said it does. Client-side parses (`kanea plan`/`run`, MCP's spec tools) fetch the node's variables over the API best-effort: a spec whose variables all resolve locally parses exactly as offline as it does today, and an unknown-variable error after a failed fetch says the fetch failed. One casualty, taken honestly: **the v1.51/v1.61 probe-skip is gone**; `variables` is a file-only half with no flag, so a version that reads it must probe the file even when every flagged half is flagged; `--config off` remains the whole-file switch it has always been.

> **v1.62 amendments**: **the default control-plane reserve drops from 1 GiB to 256 MiB** (§5.2.11, §15.1, §17 R12, §21). The floor itself was never the cost: `memory.min` is a reclaim protection, not an allocation; it protects pages that exist and sets aside none that don't, which is the same property that let v1.4 reject `mlock`. The cost lives entirely in the other half of §5.2.11, the workload ceiling (`memory.max = total RAM − system_reserve_memory`), where a full gigabyte was withheld from workloads on every node: including the majority whose control plane resides in a fraction of it. The new default covers the control plane of a node that does not build: kanead (idle RSS ≤ 150 MiB, §21), `kanea-edge`, and containerd (~42 MiB, spike measurements). It deliberately does **not** cover `buildkitd`, whose ~157 MiB permanent residency (spike ④) belongs only to nodes that run pipelines: **a build node raises `kanea init --reserve`** (512 MiB is the honest build-node number), because a floor below actual control-plane RSS protects only what fits under it and leaves the rest to OOM-killer odds; the failure R12 exists to prevent. §21's ≤ 1 GiB total-platform budget is unchanged and now explicitly decoupled from the default reserve: the budget is a ceiling on what the platform may consume, the reserve is a floor the node sets aside for it, and they were one number only by v1.4's coincidence. The knob is unchanged, and nothing migrates in place: existing nodes keep the units they have until a re-init rewrites them.

> **v1.61 amendments**: **`bind.api_addr` becomes the second thing `kanea.hcl` really carries, and the listener's TLS speaks §7.3's vocabulary** (§15.1, §13.1, §7.3, §16.2). The stanza has been in this document's sketch since v1.0; v1.18 said "where the API listens" is what config should decide, and then every implementation put it in argv: `kanea init` renders `--listen` into the kanead unit, so moving the dashboard to a network address means a re-init or a hand-edited `ExecStart` that the next init regenerates; exactly the failure v1.51 built the file to end, on exactly the kind of setting it named as the file's future. So `kanead` now reads the **`bind`** stanza: `api_addr`, **`api_tls`**, `api_domain`, and `api_cert`/`api_key`. **`api_tls` is R20's mode set applied to the node's own listener**; the platform already knows four ways to hold a certificate, and inventing a fifth spelling for the dashboard would be two vocabularies for one concept: **`acme`** requires `api_domain` (an IP cannot hold an ACME certificate) and rides the ordinary §7.3 pass as a synthetic request no spec wrote (the same account, the same HTTP-01-through-the-edge or DNS-01, the same renewal loop) with one structural difference: this is the one listener whose material renews behind its own socket, so it is served through a certificate the pass hot-swaps rather than one loaded at construction, and no SNI is required (a dashboard is dialled by IP; the edge's unknown-SNI refusal is about picking among many certificates, and this listener has one). **`self-signed`** issues from the node's own CA: the same one `kanea ca show` installs on devices, so a dashboard at a bare IP gets a real green padlock on every device that already trusts the node; the certificate names `api_domain` when given, else the host of `api_addr`, **as an IP SAN when that host is an IP** (the CA previously only ever named DNS SANs, because service domains are DNS-1123 by construction), and an unspecified host (`0.0.0.0`, `::`, none) requires `api_domain`, because a certificate needs a name and "every interface" is not one. **`provided`** is the pair of files, loaded once at construction exactly as before: a certbot renewal restarts kanead, stated rather than discovered. **`plaintext`** is allowed beyond loopback *because it was typed*: logged loudly at startup, and it implies the insecure-cookie posture on a non-loopback listener, because a Secure cookie over plain HTTP is a login that silently fails; a control that cannot act, carried, which R21 exists to refuse. An **unset `api_tls` resolves**: a declared pair means `provided`, a loopback address means `plaintext`, and anything else refuses; v1.45's posture byte for byte. Contradictions are parse errors with file and line: `plaintext` beside a pair (a dropped control), `provided` without one, `acme` without `api_domain`, a pair beside a managed mode; `acme` on a node with no `--acme-email` refuses at startup, where the subsystem's absence is known. The listener's certificate **never enters the edge bundle**: the edge serves services, and a bundle entry no route names would be dead weight with a private key in it. The v1.51 doctrine applies unchanged, half by half: **an explicit `--listen` wins and the stanza is not consulted**; the new spelling **`--listen none`** is the explicit socket-only (init's own vocabulary, chosen over the other flags' `off` because `none` is what the init prompt has always accepted), and the listen half is atomic; whichever source supplies the address supplies its TLS story; the flags keep their pre-v1.61 vocabulary (a pair or loopback), because four modes as five flags is the config file's job. No flag and no stanza is socket-only, byte for byte today's posture, and the beyond-loopback refusal for the flag path does not move from the daemon's listener construction. The sketch's `edge_http`/`edge_https` stay sketch: schema-accepted, **warned by name when set**; inside a read stanza the not-silently-swallowed rule needs the warning, since erroring on the document's own sketch would refuse v1.51's stated contract. **`kanea init` consults the file before it prompts**: a `bind.api_addr` in `kanea.hcl` (no explicit `--listen` on init's own argv) skips the listen question, renders **no** listen flags into the unit (the file owns the listener, and a unit that repeated it would turn the file off) and says so in the summary. And the v1.51 probe-skip narrows honestly: skipping the file now requires **all three** halves flagged; a node carrying only the two v1.51 flags beside a `kanea.hcl` it never wanted read should say `--config off`, which has always been the whole-file switch.

> **v1.60 amendments**: **the CLI ships through Homebrew, and the formula is written by the release workflow, never by hand** (§5.2.12, §15.4, §20 M10). Releases now publish `darwin_{amd64,arm64}` archives beside the Linux ones: the same static binary, cross-compiled with CGO off; everything Linux-only about the platform (containerd, netns, eBPF, systemd) is runtime behaviour, not build-time, so the darwin artefact costs one loop iteration and no build tags. **What a Mac gets is the authoring half and nothing more**: `kanea plan`'s parse and R-rule validation (file-and-line diagnostics, produced before any dial), spec authoring, and `kanea version`; the daemons, `init`, `install` and every socket-backed verb remain Linux, and nothing else is claimed; `install.sh` still refuses a non-Linux uname, unchanged, because a *node* install is still the script's job. The new archives ride the existing `checksums.txt` glob, so the one cosign signature extends over them with no second signing step (v1.30's bundle rule). **The formula lives in this repository** (`Formula/kanea.rb`; the tap is the repo itself): a separate `homebrew-*` repository is the Homebrew convention and was rejected as a second repository plus a cross-repo credential to rot, for the sake of one file. **`scripts/update-formula.sh` is the formula's only author**: the release workflow regenerates it from the tag and the release's own `checksums.txt` after publishing, so the version and hashes are never typed and the asset names are read out of the checksum file itself; the release.yml/install.sh/`assetName` naming contract gains a generated reader, not a fourth hand-maintained copy.
